<td style="border:0; padding:0; vertical-align:middle;"><span typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/d/d2/Blank.png" decoding="async" width="22" height="15" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="3" data-file-height="2" /></span></span>

</tbody></table>
</td></tr></tbody></table></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Today part of</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Sudan" title="Sudan">Sudan</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Eritrea" title="Eritrea">Eritrea</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Alodia</b>, also known as <b>Alwa</b> (<a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a>: <span title="Ancient Greek (to 1453)-language text"><span lang="grc">Aρουα</span></span>, <i>Aroua</i>;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELajtar200993–94_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELajtar200993–94-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Arabic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Arabic language">Arabic</a>: <span lang="ar" dir="rtl">علوة</span>, <i>ʿAlwa</i>), was a <a href="/wiki/Medieval" class="mw-redirect" title="Medieval">medieval</a> kingdom in what is now central and southern <a href="/wiki/Sudan" title="Sudan">Sudan</a>. Its capital was the city of <a href="/wiki/Soba_(city)" title="Soba (city)">Soba</a>, located near modern-day <a href="/wiki/Khartoum" title="Khartoum">Khartoum</a> at the confluence of the <a href="/wiki/Blue_Nile" title="Blue Nile">Blue</a> and <a href="/wiki/White_Nile" title="White Nile">White Nile</a> rivers.
</p><p>Founded sometime after the ancient <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kush" title="Kingdom of Kush">Kingdom of Kush</a> fell, around 350 AD, Alodia is first mentioned in historical records in 569. It was the last of the three Nubian kingdoms to convert to <a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a> in 580, following <a href="/wiki/Nobadia" class="mw-redirect" title="Nobadia">Nobadia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Makuria" title="Makuria">Makuria</a>. It possibly reached its peak during the 9th–12th centuries when records show that it exceeded its northern neighbor, Makuria, with which it maintained close dynastic ties, in size, military power and economic prosperity. Being a large, multicultural state, Alodia was administered by a powerful king and provincial governors appointed by him. The capital Soba, described as a town of "extensive dwellings and churches full of gold and gardens",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199120_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199120-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> prospered as a trading hub. Goods arrived from Makuria, the Middle East, western Africa, India and even China. Literacy in both <a href="/wiki/Nubian_languages" title="Nubian languages">Nubian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Medieval_Greek" title="Medieval Greek">Greek</a> flourished.
</p><p>From the 12th, and especially the 13th century, Alodia was declining, possibly because of invasions from the south, droughts and a shift of trade routes. In the 14th century, the country might have been ravaged by the <a href="/wiki/Plague_(disease)" title="Plague (disease)">plague</a>, while <a href="/wiki/Arab" class="mw-redirect" title="Arab">Arab</a> tribes began to migrate into the <a href="/wiki/Nile#In_Sudan" title="Nile">Upper Nile valley</a>. By around 1500 Soba had fallen to either Arabs or the <a href="/wiki/Funj" class="mw-redirect" title="Funj">Funj</a>. This likely marked the end of Alodia, although some Sudanese oral traditions claimed that it survived in the form of the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Fazughli" title="Kingdom of Fazughli">Kingdom of Fazughli</a> within the <a href="/wiki/Ethiopia" title="Ethiopia">Ethiopian</a>–Sudanese borderlands. After the destruction of Soba, the Funj established the <a href="/wiki/Sultanate_of_Sennar" class="mw-redirect" title="Sultanate of Sennar">Sultanate of Sennar</a>, ushering in a period of <a href="/wiki/Islamization" class="mw-redirect" title="Islamization">Islamization</a> and <a href="/wiki/Arabization" title="Arabization">Arabization</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Sources">Sources</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alodia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Sources">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Soba._Ruins_of_the_Christian_Church.png" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Old photo of a man looking at a capital" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Soba._Ruins_of_the_Christian_Church.png/240px-Soba._Ruins_of_the_Christian_Church.png" decoding="async" width="240" height="172" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Soba._Ruins_of_the_Christian_Church.png/360px-Soba._Ruins_of_the_Christian_Church.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Soba._Ruins_of_the_Christian_Church.png/480px-Soba._Ruins_of_the_Christian_Church.png 2x" data-file-width="745" data-file-height="534" /></a><figcaption>A <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Egyptian_Sudan" title="Anglo-Egyptian Sudan">British colonial official</a> inspecting the ruins of <a href="/wiki/Soba_(city)" title="Soba (city)">Soba</a>, 1905</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Documenting_excavations_at_Soba_(Sudan).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A photo of the site of Soba" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Documenting_excavations_at_Soba_%28Sudan%29.jpg/290px-Documenting_excavations_at_Soba_%28Sudan%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="290" height="163" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Documenting_excavations_at_Soba_%28Sudan%29.jpg/435px-Documenting_excavations_at_Soba_%28Sudan%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Documenting_excavations_at_Soba_%28Sudan%29.jpg/580px-Documenting_excavations_at_Soba_%28Sudan%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5472" data-file-height="3078" /></a><figcaption>Soba during the excavations in late 2019</figcaption></figure>
<p>Alodia is by far the least studied of the three medieval <a href="/wiki/Nubia" title="Nubia">Nubian</a> kingdoms,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2014183_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2014183-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> hence evidence is very slim.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2014197_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2014197-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Most of what is known about it comes from a handful of medieval Arabic historians. The most important of these are the <a href="/wiki/Geography_and_cartography_in_medieval_Islam" class="mw-redirect" title="Geography and cartography in medieval Islam">Islamic geographers</a> <a href="/wiki/Al-Yaqubi" class="mw-redirect" title="Al-Yaqubi">al-Yaqubi</a> (9th century), <a href="/wiki/Ibn_Hawqal" title="Ibn Hawqal">Ibn Hawqal</a> and <a href="/wiki/Al-Aswani" class="mw-redirect" title="Al-Aswani">al-Aswani</a> (10th century), who both visited the country, and the <a href="/wiki/Copts" title="Copts">Copt</a> <a href="/wiki/Abu_al-Makarim" title="Abu al-Makarim">Abu al-Makarim</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner201393_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner201393-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> (12th century).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199115–23_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199115–23-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> The events around the Christianization of the kingdom in the 6th century were described by the contemporary <a href="/wiki/Bishop" title="Bishop">bishop</a> <a href="/wiki/John_of_Ephesus" title="John of Ephesus">John of Ephesus</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199112–15_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199112–15-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> various post-medieval Sudanese sources address its fall.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002255_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002255-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVantini2006487–491_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVantini2006487–491-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Al-Aswani noted that he interacted with a <a href="/wiki/Nubians" title="Nubians">Nubian</a> historian who was "well-acquainted with the country of Alwa",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199119–20_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199119–20-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> but no medieval Nubian <a href="/wiki/Historiography" title="Historiography">historiographical</a> work has yet been discovered.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby20029_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby20029-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While many Alodian sites are known,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199158–70_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199158–70-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> only the capital <a href="/wiki/Soba_(city)" title="Soba (city)">Soba</a> has been extensively excavated.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner201325_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner201325-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Parts of this site were unearthed in the early 1950s, further excavations taking place in the 1980s and 1990s.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards2004221_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards2004221-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> A new multidisciplinary research project was scheduled to start in late 2019.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDrzewieckiCedroRyndziewiczKhogli_Ali_Ahmed201828_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrzewieckiCedroRyndziewiczKhogli_Ali_Ahmed201828-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Soba is approximately 2.75&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (1.06&#160;sq&#160;mi) in size and is covered with numerous mounds of brick rubble previously belonging to monumental structures.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards2004221_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards2004221-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Discoveries made so far include several churches, a palace, cemeteries and numerous small finds.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013161–164_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013161–164-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geography">Geography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alodia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Geography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Alodia was located in Nubia, a region which, in the <a href="/wiki/Middle_ages" class="mw-redirect" title="Middle ages">middle ages</a>, extended from <a href="/wiki/Aswan" title="Aswan">Aswan</a> in southern <a href="/wiki/Egypt" title="Egypt">Egypt</a> to an undetermined point south of the confluence of the <a href="/wiki/White_Nile" title="White Nile">White</a> and <a href="/wiki/Blue_Nile" title="Blue Nile">Blue Nile</a> rivers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner201328–29_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner201328–29-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> The heartland of the kingdom was the <a href="/wiki/Al_Jazirah_(state)" class="mw-redirect" title="Al Jazirah (state)">Gezira</a>, a fertile plain bounded by the White Nile in the west and the Blue Nile in the east.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199141_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199141-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> In contrast to the White Nile Valley, the Blue Nile Valley is rich in known Alodian archaeological sites, among them Soba.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2014Figure_2_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2014Figure_2-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> The extent of the Alodian influence to the south is unclear,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEObluski201715_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEObluski201715-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> although it is likely that it bordered the <a href="/wiki/Ethiopia" title="Ethiopia">Ethiopian</a> highlands.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels19918_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels19918-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> The southernmost known Alodian sites are in the proximity of <a href="/wiki/Sennar" title="Sennar">Sennar</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-fn2_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fn2-27">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>To the west of the White Nile, Ibn Hawqal differentiated between Al-Jeblien, which was controlled by <a href="/wiki/Makuria" title="Makuria">Makuria</a> and probably corresponded with northern <a href="/wiki/Kordofan" title="Kordofan">Kordofan</a>, and the Alodian-controlled Al-Ahdin, which has been identified with the <a href="/wiki/Nuba_Mountains" title="Nuba Mountains">Nuba Mountains</a>, and perhaps extended as far south as <a href="/w/index.php?title=Jebel_al_Liri&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Jebel al Liri (page does not exist)">Jebel al Liri</a>, near the modern border to <a href="/wiki/South_Sudan" title="South Sudan">South Sudan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESpaulding199849_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpaulding199849-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Nubian connections with <a href="/wiki/Darfur" title="Darfur">Darfur</a> have been suggested, but evidence is lacking.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards2004253_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards2004253-29">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The northern region of Alodia probably extended from the confluence of the two Niles downstream to <a href="/wiki/Abu_Hamad" title="Abu Hamad">Abu Hamad</a> near <a href="/w/index.php?title=Mograt_Island&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Mograt Island (page does not exist)">Mograt Island</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199174_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199174-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> Abu Hamad likely constituted the northernmost outpost of the Alodian province known as al-Abwab ("the gates"),<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199121–22_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199121–22-31">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> although some scholars also suggest a more southerly location, nearer the <a href="/wiki/Atbara_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Atbara River">Atbara River</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby200226_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby200226-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> No evidence for a major Alodian settlement has been discovered north of the confluence of the two Niles,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2014192_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2014192-33">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> although several forts have been recorded there.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2014188–190_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2014188–190-34">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Lying between the <a href="/wiki/Nile" title="Nile">Nile</a> and the Atbara was the <a href="/wiki/Butana" title="Butana">Butana</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199162_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199162-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> grassland suitable for <a href="/wiki/Pastoralism" title="Pastoralism">livestock</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199174_30-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199174-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> Along the Atbara and the adjacent <a href="/wiki/Mareb_River" title="Mareb River">Gash Delta</a> (near <a href="/wiki/Kassala" title="Kassala">Kassala</a>) many Christian sites have been noted.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2014187_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2014187-36">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> According to Ibn Hawqal, a vassal king loyal to Alodia governed the region around the Gash Delta.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199198_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199198-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> In fact, much of the Sudanese-Ethiopian-<a href="/wiki/Eritrea" title="Eritrea">Eritrean</a> borderlands, once under control of the Ethiopian <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Aksum" title="Kingdom of Aksum">Kingdom of Aksum</a>, appear to have been under Alodian influence.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFattovich1984105–106_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFattovich1984105–106-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> The accounts of both Ibn Hawqal and al-Aswani suggest that Alodia also controlled the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_desert" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern desert">desert</a> along the <a href="/wiki/Red_Sea" title="Red Sea">Red Sea</a> coast.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels19918_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels19918-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alodia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: History">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Origins">Origins</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alodia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Origins">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The name Alodia might be of considerable antiquity, perhaps appearing first as <i>Alut</i> on a <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kush" title="Kingdom of Kush">Kushite</a> <a href="/wiki/Stela" class="mw-redirect" title="Stela">stela</a> from the late 4th century BC. It appeared again as <i>Alwa</i> on a list of Kushite towns by the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire">Roman</a> author <a href="/wiki/Pliny_the_Elder" title="Pliny the Elder">Pliny the Elder</a> (1st century AD), said to be located south of <a href="/wiki/Mero%C3%AB" title="Meroë">Meroë</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug19918_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug19918-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Another town named <i>Alwa</i> is mentioned in a 4th-century Aksumite inscription, this time located near the confluence of the Nile and the Atbara rivers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHatke2013§4.5.2.3_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHatke2013§4.5.2.3-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Gefesselter_nubischer_K%C3%B6nig.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Bronze figurine of a bound man." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Gefesselter_nubischer_K%C3%B6nig.JPG/220px-Gefesselter_nubischer_K%C3%B6nig.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Gefesselter_nubischer_K%C3%B6nig.JPG/330px-Gefesselter_nubischer_K%C3%B6nig.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Gefesselter_nubischer_K%C3%B6nig.JPG/440px-Gefesselter_nubischer_K%C3%B6nig.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1394" data-file-height="1046" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kush" title="Kingdom of Kush">Kushite</a> bronze figurine, 1st century BC. The <a href="/wiki/Meroitic_language" title="Meroitic language">Meroitic</a> inscription on its underside identifies the captive individual as a <a href="/wiki/Nubians" title="Nubians">Nubian</a> king.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERilly2008Fig._3_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERilly2008Fig._3-41">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>By the early 4th century the Kingdom of Kush, which used to control much of Sudan's riverbanks, was in decline, and Nubians (speakers of <a href="/wiki/Nubian_languages" title="Nubian languages">Nubian languages</a>) began to settle in the Nile Valley.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERilly2008211_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERilly2008211-42">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> They originally lived west of the Nile, but changes in the climate forced them eastward, resulting in conflicts with Kush from at least the 1st-century BC.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERilly2008216–217_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERilly2008216–217-43">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> In the mid-4th century the Nubians occupied most of the area once controlled by Kush,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHatke2013§4.5.2.3_40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHatke2013§4.5.2.3-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> while it was limited to the northern reaches of the Butana.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner201335_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner201335-44">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> An Aksumite inscription mentions how the warlike Nubians also threatened the borders of the Aksumite kingdom north of the <a href="/wiki/Tekez%C3%A9_River" title="Tekezé River">Tekeze River</a>, resulting in an Aksumite expedition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHatke2013§4.5.2.1.,_see_also_§4.5._for_the_discussion_of_a_Greek_inscription_with_similar_content_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHatke2013§4.5.2.1.,_see_also_§4.5._for_the_discussion_of_a_Greek_inscription_with_similar_content-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> It describes a Nubian defeat by Aksumite forces and a subsequent march to the confluence of the Nile and Atbara. There the Aksumites plundered several Kushite towns, including Alwa.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHatke2013§4.5.2.3_40-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHatke2013§4.5.2.3-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Early_medieval_fort_at_Jebel_Umm_Marrahi_(Sudan).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Aerial photography of fort remains" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1d/Early_medieval_fort_at_Jebel_Umm_Marrahi_%28Sudan%29.jpg/220px-Early_medieval_fort_at_Jebel_Umm_Marrahi_%28Sudan%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="140" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1d/Early_medieval_fort_at_Jebel_Umm_Marrahi_%28Sudan%29.jpg/330px-Early_medieval_fort_at_Jebel_Umm_Marrahi_%28Sudan%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1d/Early_medieval_fort_at_Jebel_Umm_Marrahi_%28Sudan%29.jpg/440px-Early_medieval_fort_at_Jebel_Umm_Marrahi_%28Sudan%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3969" data-file-height="2520" /></a><figcaption>The remains of the fortress of Umm Marrahi near <a href="/wiki/Omdurman" title="Omdurman">Omdurman</a>, dating to the 6th century</figcaption></figure>
<p>Archaeological evidence suggests the Kingdom of Kush ceased to exist in the middle of the 4th century. It is not known whether the Aksumite expeditions played a direct role in its fall. It seems likely that the Aksumite presence in Nubia was short-lived.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHatke2013§4.6.3_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHatke2013§4.6.3-46">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Eventually, the region saw the development of regional centers whose ruling elites were buried in large <a href="/wiki/Tumulus" title="Tumulus">tumuli</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby200222–23_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby200222–23-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Such tumuli, within what would become Alodia, are known from <a href="/wiki/El-Hobagi" title="El-Hobagi">El-Hobagi</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Jebel_Qisi&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Jebel Qisi (page does not exist)">Jebel Qisi</a> and perhaps <a href="/wiki/Jebel_Aulia_Dam" title="Jebel Aulia Dam">Jebel Aulia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2014191_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2014191-48">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> The excavated tumuli of El-Hobagi are known to date to the late 4th century,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby200228_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby200228-49">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> and contained an assortment of weaponry imitating Kushite royal funerary rituals.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby200240–41_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby200240–41-50">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Meanwhile, many Kushite temples and settlements, including the former capital Meroë, seem to have been largely abandoned.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards2004187_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards2004187-51">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> The Kushites themselves were absorbed into the Nubians<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner201339_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner201339-52">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> and their <a href="/wiki/Meroitic_language" title="Meroitic language">language</a> was replaced by Nubian.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards2004182_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards2004182-53">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>How the Kingdom of Alodia came into being is unknown.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner201345_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner201345-54">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Its formation was completed by the mid-6th century, when it is said to have existed alongside the other Nubian kingdoms of <a href="/wiki/Nobadia" class="mw-redirect" title="Nobadia">Nobadia</a> and Makuria in the north.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby200226_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby200226-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Soba, which by the 6th century had developed into a major urban center,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby199820_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby199820-55">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> served as its capital.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby200226_32-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby200226-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> In 569 the Kingdom of Alodia was mentioned for the first time, being described by John of Ephesus as a kingdom on the cusp of <a href="/wiki/Christianization" title="Christianization">Christianization</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner201345_54-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner201345-54">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Independently of John of Ephesus, the kingdom's existence is also verified by a late 6th century <a href="/wiki/Medieval_Greek" title="Medieval Greek">Greek</a> document from <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Egypt" class="mw-redirect" title="Byzantine Egypt">Byzantine Egypt</a>, describing the sale of an Alodian slave girl.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPierce1995148–166_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPierce1995148–166-56">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Christianization_and_peak">Christianization and peak</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alodia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Christianization and peak">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Greek_cross,_Musawwarat_es-Sufra_(Sudan).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Graffito in the form a cross carved into a brick." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Greek_cross%2C_Musawwarat_es-Sufra_%28Sudan%29.jpg/220px-Greek_cross%2C_Musawwarat_es-Sufra_%28Sudan%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="183" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Greek_cross%2C_Musawwarat_es-Sufra_%28Sudan%29.jpg/330px-Greek_cross%2C_Musawwarat_es-Sufra_%28Sudan%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d9/Greek_cross%2C_Musawwarat_es-Sufra_%28Sudan%29.jpg/440px-Greek_cross%2C_Musawwarat_es-Sufra_%28Sudan%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="556" data-file-height="463" /></a><figcaption>Cross <a href="/wiki/Graffito_(archaeology)" title="Graffito (archaeology)">graffito</a> from <a href="/wiki/Musawwarat_es-Sufra" title="Musawwarat es-Sufra">Musawwarat es-Sufra</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Michael_monograms,_Meroe_quarry.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fc/Michael_monograms%2C_Meroe_quarry.jpg/220px-Michael_monograms%2C_Meroe_quarry.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="248" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fc/Michael_monograms%2C_Meroe_quarry.jpg/330px-Michael_monograms%2C_Meroe_quarry.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fc/Michael_monograms%2C_Meroe_quarry.jpg/440px-Michael_monograms%2C_Meroe_quarry.jpg 2x" data-file-width="503" data-file-height="567" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Monogram" title="Monogram">Monograms</a> of <a href="/wiki/Archangel_Michael" class="mw-redirect" title="Archangel Michael">Archangel Michael</a> in a quarry near Meroë. Such monograms seem to have been the most popular epigraphic expression in Alodia, while longer inscriptions are comparably rare.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETsakosKleinitz2018127_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETsakosKleinitz2018127-57">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>John of Ephesus' account describes the events around the Christianization of Alodia in detail. As the southernmost of the three Nubian kingdoms, Alodia was the last to be converted to Christianity. According to John, the Alodian King was aware of the conversion of Nobadia in 543 and asked him to send a bishop who would also baptize his people. The request was granted in 580 and <a href="/wiki/Longinus_(missionary)" title="Longinus (missionary)">Longinus</a> was sent, leading to the baptism of the King, his family and the local nobility. Thus, Alodia became a part of the Christian world under the <a href="/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church_of_Alexandria" class="mw-redirect" title="Coptic Orthodox Church of Alexandria">Coptic Patriarchate of Alexandria</a>. After conversion, several pagan temples, such as the one in <a href="/wiki/Musawwarat_es-Sufra" title="Musawwarat es-Sufra">Musawwarat es-Sufra</a>, were probably converted into churches.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner201351–62_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner201351–62-58">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> The extent and speed with which Christianity spread among the Alodian populace is uncertain. Despite the conversion of the nobility, it is likely that Christianization of the rural population progressed only slowly, if at all.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards200195_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards200195-59">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> John of Ephesus' report also implies tensions between Alodia and Makuria. Several forts north of the confluence of the two Niles have recently been dated to this period. However, their occupation did not exceed the 7th century, suggesting that the Makurian-Alodian conflict was soon resolved.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDrzewieckiCedro2019129_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrzewieckiCedro2019129-60">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Between 639 and 641, Muslim Arabs <a href="/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_Egypt" class="mw-redirect" title="Muslim conquest of Egypt">conquered Egypt</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Byzantine_Empire" title="Byzantine Empire">Byzantine Empire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby200268_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby200268-61">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Makuria, which by this time had been unified with Nobadia,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner201377_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner201377-62">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> fended off two subsequent <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Muslim</a> invasions, one in <a href="/wiki/First_Battle_of_Dongola" title="First Battle of Dongola">641/642</a> and another in <a href="/wiki/Second_Battle_of_Dongola" title="Second Battle of Dongola">652</a>. In the aftermath, Makuria and the Arabs agreed to sign the <i><a href="/wiki/Baqt" title="Baqt">Baqt</a></i>, a peace treaty that included a yearly exchange of gifts and socioeconomic regulations between Arabs and Nubians.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby200268–71_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby200268–71-63">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Alodia was explicitly mentioned in the treaty as not being affected by it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby200277_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby200277-64">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> While the Arabs failed to conquer Nubia, they began to settle along the western coast of the Red Sea. They founded the port towns of <a href="/wiki/Aydhab" class="mw-redirect" title="Aydhab">Aydhab</a> and <a href="/wiki/Badi,_Sudan" title="Badi, Sudan">Badi</a> in the 7th century and <a href="/wiki/Suakin" title="Suakin">Suakin</a>, first mentioned in the 10th century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPower2008_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPower2008-65">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> From the 9th century, they pushed further inland, settling among the <a href="/wiki/Beja_people" title="Beja people">Beja</a> throughout the Eastern Desert. Arab influence would remain confined to the east of the Nile until the 14th century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdams1977553–554_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams1977553–554-66">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Nubia_on_al-Idrisi%27s_map_(12th_century).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A Medieval map showing the Nile region below Egypt" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d3/Nubia_on_al-Idrisi%27s_map_%2812th_century%29.jpg/220px-Nubia_on_al-Idrisi%27s_map_%2812th_century%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="280" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d3/Nubia_on_al-Idrisi%27s_map_%2812th_century%29.jpg/330px-Nubia_on_al-Idrisi%27s_map_%2812th_century%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d3/Nubia_on_al-Idrisi%27s_map_%2812th_century%29.jpg/440px-Nubia_on_al-Idrisi%27s_map_%2812th_century%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="453" data-file-height="577" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/South-up_map_orientation" title="South-up map orientation">South-up map</a> of Nubia by <a href="/wiki/Al-Idrisi" class="mw-redirect" title="Al-Idrisi">al-Idrisi</a> (1192 AD). Alodia ("galua") is erroneously depicted as being north of <a href="/wiki/Makuria" title="Makuria">Makuria</a> ("domkola", after <a href="/wiki/Old_Dongola" title="Old Dongola">Dongola</a>, the Makurian capital).</figcaption></figure>
<p>Based on the archaeological evidence it has been suggested that Alodia's capital Soba underwent its peak development between the 9th and 12th centuries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEShinnie196176_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShinnie196176-67">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> In the 9th century, Alodia was, albeit briefly, described for the first time by the Arab historian al-Yaqubi. In his short account, Alodia is said to be the stronger of the two Nubian kingdoms, being a country requiring a three-month journey to cross. He also recorded that Muslims would occasionally travel there.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199116–17_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199116–17-68">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A century later, in the mid-10th century, Alodia was visited by traveler and historian Ibn Hawqal, resulting in the most comprehensive known account of the kingdom. He described the geography and people of Alodia in considerable detail, giving the impression of a large, polyethnic state. He also noted its prosperity, having an "uninterrupted chain of villages and a continuous strip of cultivated lands".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199117–19_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199117–19-69">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> When Ibn Hawqal arrived, the ruling king was named Eusebius, who was, upon his death, succeeded by his nephew Stephanos.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199117_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199117-70">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002261_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002261-71">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> Another Alodian king from this period was David, who is known from a tombstone in Soba. His rule was initially dated to 999–1015, but based on <a href="/wiki/Paleography" class="mw-redirect" title="Paleography">paleographical</a> grounds it is now dated more broadly, to the 9th or 10th centuries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELajtar2003203_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELajtar2003203-72">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ibn Hawqal's report describing Alodia's geography was largely confirmed by al-Aswani, a <a href="/wiki/Fatimids" class="mw-redirect" title="Fatimids">Fatimid</a> ambassador sent to Makuria, who went on to travel to Alodia. In a similar manner to al-Yaqubi's description of 100 years before, Alodia was noted as being more powerful than Makuria, more extensive and having a larger army. The capital Soba was a prosperous town with "fine buildings, and extensive dwellings and churches full of gold and gardens", while also having a large Muslim quarter.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199120_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199120-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:King_Moses_George_of_Makuria.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A painting of a man wearing red and white regal clothing and a crown." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/King_Moses_George_of_Makuria.jpg/220px-King_Moses_George_of_Makuria.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="234" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/King_Moses_George_of_Makuria.jpg/330px-King_Moses_George_of_Makuria.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/King_Moses_George_of_Makuria.jpg/440px-King_Moses_George_of_Makuria.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2400" data-file-height="2552" /></a><figcaption>King Mouses Georgios, who probably ruled Makuria and Alodia simultaneously. <a href="/wiki/Faras" title="Faras">Faras</a>, late 12th century.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Abu al-Makarim (12th century)<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner201393_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner201393-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> was the last historian to refer to Alodia in detail. It was still described as a large, Christian kingdom housing around 400 churches. A particularly large and finely constructed one was said to be located in Soba, called the "Church of Manbali".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199122–23_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199122–23-73">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Two Alodian kings, Basil and Paul, are mentioned in 12th century Arabic letters from <a href="/wiki/Qasr_Ibrim" title="Qasr Ibrim">Qasr Ibrim</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002261_71-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002261-71">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There is evidence that at certain periods there were close relations between the Alodian and the Makurian royal families. It is possible that the throne frequently passed to a king whose father was of the royal family of the other state.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby200289_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby200289-74">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Nubiologist <a href="/w/index.php?title=W%C5%82odzimierz_Godlewski&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Włodzimierz Godlewski (page does not exist)">Włodzimierz Godlewski</a> states that it was under the Makurian king <a href="/wiki/Merkurios_of_Makuria" title="Merkurios of Makuria">Merkurios</a> (early 8th century) that the two kingdoms began to approach each other.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGodlewski2012204_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGodlewski2012204-75">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> In 943 <a href="/wiki/Al-Masudi" title="Al-Masudi">al Masudi</a> wrote that the Makurian king ruled over Alodia, while Ibn Hawqal wrote that it was the other way around.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby200289_74-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby200289-74">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> The 11th century saw the appearance of a new royal crown in Makurian art; it has been suggested that this derived from the Alodian court.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDanysZielinska2017184_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDanysZielinska2017184-76">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> King <a href="/wiki/Moses_Georgios_of_Makuria" title="Moses Georgios of Makuria">Mouses Georgios</a>, who is known to have ruled in Makuria in the second half of the 12th century, most likely ruled both kingdoms via a <a href="/wiki/Personal_union" title="Personal union">personal union</a>. Considering that in his royal title ("king of the Arouades and Makuritai") Alodia is mentioned before Makuria, he might have initially been an Alodian king.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELajtar200989–94_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELajtar200989–94-77">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> 
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Decline">Decline</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alodia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Decline">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Incense_burner_made_of_bronze,_Soba.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A bronze incense burner with a Nubian inscription" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Incense_burner_made_of_bronze%2C_Soba.jpg/310px-Incense_burner_made_of_bronze%2C_Soba.jpg" decoding="async" width="310" height="139" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Incense_burner_made_of_bronze%2C_Soba.jpg/465px-Incense_burner_made_of_bronze%2C_Soba.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0e/Incense_burner_made_of_bronze%2C_Soba.jpg/620px-Incense_burner_made_of_bronze%2C_Soba.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1592" data-file-height="714" /></a><figcaption>Bronze incense burner, bearing a damaged <a href="/wiki/Coptic_language" title="Coptic language">Coptic</a> inscription. Allegedly discovered in Soba.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Archaeological evidence from Soba suggests a decline of the town, and therefore possibly the Alodian kingdom, from the 12th century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002252_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002252-78">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> By <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1300 the decline of Alodia was well advanced.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;FaheySpaulding197419_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;FaheySpaulding197419-79">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> No pottery or glassware postdating the 13th century has been identified at Soba.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels199134_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels199134-80">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Two churches were apparently destroyed during the 13th century, although they were rebuilt shortly afterwards.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels19919_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels19919-81">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> It has been suggested that Alodia was under attack by an African, possibly <a href="/wiki/Nilotes" class="mw-redirect" title="Nilotes">Nilotic</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBeswick200424_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeswick200424-82">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> people called Damadim who originated from the border region of modern Sudan and South Sudan, along the <a href="/wiki/Bahr_el_Ghazal_River" title="Bahr el Ghazal River">Bahr el Ghazal River</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013115_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013115-83">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> According to geographer <a href="/wiki/Ibn_Sa%27id_al-Maghribi" title="Ibn Sa&#39;id al-Maghribi">Ibn Sa'id al-Maghribi</a>, they attacked Nubia in 1220.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVantini1975400_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVantini1975400-84">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> Soba may have been conquered at this time, suffering occupation and destruction.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013115_83-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013115-83">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> In the late 13th century, another invasion by an unspecified people from the south occurred.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHasan1967130_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasan1967130-85">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> In the same period poet <a href="/w/index.php?title=Al-Harrani_(poet)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Al-Harrani (poet) (page does not exist)">al-Harrani</a> wrote that Alodia's capital was now called Waylula,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;FaheySpaulding197419_79-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;FaheySpaulding197419-79">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> described as "very large" and "built on the west bank of the Nile".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVantini1975448_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVantini1975448-86">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> In the early 14th century geographer <a href="/wiki/Al-Dimashqi_(geographer)" title="Al-Dimashqi (geographer)">Shamsaddin al-Dimashqi</a> wrote that the capital was a place named Kusha, located far from the Nile, where water had to be obtained from wells.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdams1977537–538_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams1977537–538-87">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> The contemporary <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italian</a>-<a href="/wiki/Mallorca" title="Mallorca">Mallorcan</a> <a href="/wiki/Angelino_Dulcert" title="Angelino Dulcert">Dulcert</a> map features both Alodia ("Coale") and Soba ("Sobaa").<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHirsch199088_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHirsch199088-88">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Economic factors also seem to have played a part in Alodia's decline. From the 10th to 12th centuries the <a href="/wiki/East_Africa" title="East Africa">East African</a> coast saw the rise of new trading cities such as <a href="/wiki/Kilwa" class="mw-redirect" title="Kilwa">Kilwa</a>. These were direct mercantile competitors since they exported similar goods to Nubia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrajetzki2009121–122_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrajetzki2009121–122-89">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> A period of severe droughts occurring in Sub-Saharan Africa between 1150 and 1500 would have affected the Nubian economy as well.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZurawski201484_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZurawski201484-90">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Paleoethnobotany" title="Paleoethnobotany">Archeobotanical</a> evidence from Soba suggests the town suffered from <a href="/wiki/Overgrazing" title="Overgrazing">overgrazing</a> and overcultivation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECartwright1999256_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECartwright1999256-91">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By 1276 al-Abwab, previously described as the northernmost Alodian province, was recorded as an <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_al-Abwab" title="Kingdom of al-Abwab">independent splinter kingdom</a> ruling over vast territories. The precise circumstances of its secession and its relations with Alodia thereafter remain unknown.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002254_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002254-92">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> Based on pottery finds it has been suggested that al-Abwab continued to thrive until the 15th and perhaps even the 16th century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013127,_159_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013127,_159-93">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> In 1286 a <a href="/wiki/Mamluk_Sultanate_(Cairo)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mamluk Sultanate (Cairo)">Mamluke</a> prince sent messengers to several rulers in central Sudan. It is not clear if they were still subject to the king in Soba<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199199_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199199-94">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> or if they were independent, implying a fragmentation of Alodia into multiple petty states by the late 13th century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;FaheySpaulding197419_79-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;FaheySpaulding197419-79">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> In 1317 a Mamluk expedition pursued Arab brigands as far south as Kassala in Taka (one of the regions which received a Mamluk messenger in 1286<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199199_94-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199199-94">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>), marching through al-Abwab and Makuria on their return.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013138_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013138-95">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Migration_of_Arabs_into_Sudan.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A map of Sudan, Eritrea, Djibouti and northern Ethiopia." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Migration_of_Arabs_into_Sudan.jpg/350px-Migration_of_Arabs_into_Sudan.jpg" decoding="async" width="350" height="288" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Migration_of_Arabs_into_Sudan.jpg/525px-Migration_of_Arabs_into_Sudan.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Migration_of_Arabs_into_Sudan.jpg/700px-Migration_of_Arabs_into_Sudan.jpg 2x" data-file-width="818" data-file-height="674" /></a><figcaption>Map depicting the migration routes employed by the <a href="/wiki/Arab" class="mw-redirect" title="Arab">Arab</a> tribes to push into <a href="/wiki/Sudan" title="Sudan">Sudan</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>During the 14th and 15th centuries much of what is now Sudan was overrun by <a href="/wiki/Arab" class="mw-redirect" title="Arab">Arab</a> tribes and briefly conquered by the <a href="/wiki/Adal_Sultanate" title="Adal Sultanate">Adal Sultanate</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHasan1967176_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasan1967176-98">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHasan1967176_98-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasan1967176-98">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> They perhaps profited from the <a href="/wiki/Plague_(disease)" title="Plague (disease)">plague</a> which might have ravaged Nubia in the mid-14th century killing many sedentary Nubians, but not affecting the nomadic Arabs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013142–143_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013142–143-99">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> They would have then intermixed with the remaining local population, gradually taking control over land and people,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHasan1967128_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasan1967128-100">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> greatly benefiting from their large population in spreading their culture.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHasan1967175_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasan1967175-101">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> The first recorded Arab migration to Nubia dates to 1324.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHasan1967106_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasan1967106-102">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> It was the disintegration of Makuria in the late 14th century that, according to archaeologist <a href="/wiki/William_Y._Adams" title="William Y. Adams">William Y. Adams</a>, caused the "flood gates" to "burst wide open".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdams1977556_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams1977556-103">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> Many, initially coming from Egypt, followed the course of the Nile until they reached <a href="/wiki/Al_Dabbah" class="mw-redirect" title="Al Dabbah">Al Dabbah</a>. Here they headed west to migrate along the <a href="/wiki/Wadi_Al-Malik" title="Wadi Al-Malik">Wadi Al-Malik</a> to reach Darfur or Kordofan.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBraukämper1992108–109,_111_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBraukämper1992108–109,_111-104">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> Alodia, in particular the Butana and the Gezira, was the target of those Arabs who had lived among the Beja<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHasan1967145_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasan1967145-105">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> in the Eastern Desert for centuries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdams1977554_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams1977554-106">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Initially, the kingdom was able to exercise authority over some of the newly arrived Arab groups, forcing them to pay tribute. The situation grew increasingly precarious as more Arabs arrived.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHasan1967129,_132–133_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasan1967129,_132–133-107">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> By the second half of the 15th century, Arabs had settled in the entire central Sudanese Nile valley, except for the area around Soba,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHasan1967128_100-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasan1967128-100">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> which was all that was left of Alodia's domain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdams1977545_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams1977545-108">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> In 1474<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVantini1975784_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVantini1975784-109">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> it was recorded that Arabs founded the town of <a href="/wiki/Arbaji" title="Arbaji">Arbaji</a> on the Blue Nile, which would quickly develop into an important centre of commerce and Islamic learning.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcHugh199438_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcHugh199438-110">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> In around 1500 the Nubians were recorded to be in a state of total political fragmentation, as they had no king, but 150 independent lordships centered around castles on both sides of the Nile.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;FaheySpaulding197419_79-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;FaheySpaulding197419-79">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> Archaeology attests that Soba was largely ruined by this time.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002255_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002255-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Fall">Fall</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alodia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Fall">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:ArnoldvHarff3holzschn1859.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A pen and ink drawing of a mounted man wearing Arab dress and carrying a spear" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1e/ArnoldvHarff3holzschn1859.jpg/260px-ArnoldvHarff3holzschn1859.jpg" decoding="async" width="260" height="241" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1e/ArnoldvHarff3holzschn1859.jpg/390px-ArnoldvHarff3holzschn1859.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1e/ArnoldvHarff3holzschn1859.jpg/520px-ArnoldvHarff3holzschn1859.jpg 2x" data-file-width="576" data-file-height="533" /></a><figcaption>Late 15th century illustration of Arab horseman by <a href="/wiki/Arnold_von_Harff" title="Arnold von Harff">Arnold von Harff</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>It is unclear if the Kingdom of Alodia was destroyed by the Arabs under <a href="/w/index.php?title=Abdallah_Jammah&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Abdallah Jammah (page does not exist)">Abdallah Jammah</a> or by the <a href="/wiki/Funj" class="mw-redirect" title="Funj">Funj</a>, an African group from the south led by their king <a href="/wiki/Amara_Dunqas" title="Amara Dunqas">Amara Dunqas</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002255_11-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002255-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Most modern scholars agree now that it fell due to the Arabs.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199125_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199125-111">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdams1977538_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams1977538-112">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Abdallah Jammah ("Abdallah the gatherer"), the eponymous ancestor<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdams1977539_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams1977539-113">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> of the Sudanese <a href="/wiki/Abdallabi_tribe" title="Abdallabi tribe">Abdallab tribe</a>, was a <a href="/w/index.php?title=Rufa%27a_Arabs&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Rufa&#39;a Arabs (page does not exist)">Rufa'a</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHasan1967132_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasan1967132-114">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> Arab who, according to Sudanese traditions, settled in the Nile Valley after coming from the east. He consolidated his power and established his capital at <a href="/w/index.php?title=Qerri&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Qerri (page does not exist)">Qerri</a>, just north of the confluence of the two Niles.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;FaheySpaulding197423_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;FaheySpaulding197423-115">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> In the late 15th century he gathered the Arab tribes to act against the Alodian "tyranny", as it is called, which has been interpreted as having a religious-economic motive. The Muslim Arabs no longer accepted the rule of, nor taxation by, a Christian ruler. Under Abdallah's leadership Alodia and its capital Soba were destroyed,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHasan1967132–133_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasan1967132–133-116">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> resulting in rich booty such as a "bejeweled crown" and a "famous necklace of pearls and rubies".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;FaheySpaulding197423_115-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;FaheySpaulding197423-115">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tribal_scarification_of_the_Abdallabs,_Sudan.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Portrait of brown-skinned man wearing a white turban and with scarification on his cheek" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Tribal_scarification_of_the_Abdallabs%2C_Sudan.jpg/180px-Tribal_scarification_of_the_Abdallabs%2C_Sudan.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="216" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Tribal_scarification_of_the_Abdallabs%2C_Sudan.jpg/270px-Tribal_scarification_of_the_Abdallabs%2C_Sudan.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Tribal_scarification_of_the_Abdallabs%2C_Sudan.jpg/360px-Tribal_scarification_of_the_Abdallabs%2C_Sudan.jpg 2x" data-file-width="390" data-file-height="468" /></a><figcaption>An <a href="/wiki/Abdallabi_tribe" title="Abdallabi tribe">Abdallab</a> with the typical tribal <a href="/wiki/Scarification" title="Scarification">scarification</a>. The Abdallab trace their origin to <a href="/w/index.php?title=Abdallah_Jammah&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Abdallah Jammah (page does not exist)">Abdallah Jammah</a>, the alleged destroyer of Alodia.</figcaption></figure>
<p>According to another tradition recorded in old documents from <a href="/wiki/Shendi" title="Shendi">Shendi</a>, Soba was destroyed by Abdallah Jammah in 1509 having already been attacked in 1474. The idea of uniting the Arabs against Alodia is said to have already been on the mind of an <a href="/wiki/Emir" title="Emir">emir</a> who lived between 1439 and 1459. To this end, he migrated from <a href="/wiki/Barah,_Sudan" title="Barah, Sudan">Bara</a> in Kordofan to a mountain near <a href="/wiki/Ed_Dueim" title="Ed Dueim">Ed Dueim</a> on the White Nile. Under his grandson, called Emir Humaydan, the White Nile was crossed. There he met other Arab tribes and attacked Alodia. The king of Alodia was killed, but the "patriarch", probably the <a href="/wiki/Archbishop" title="Archbishop">archbishop</a> of Soba, managed to flee. He soon returned to Soba. A puppet king was crowned and an army of Nubians, Beja and <a href="/wiki/Ethiopian_Empire" title="Ethiopian Empire">Abyssinians</a> was assembled to fight "for the sake of religion". Meanwhile, the Arab alliance was about to fracture, but Abdallah Jammah reunited them, while also allying with the Funj king Amara Dunqas. Together they finally defeated and killed the patriarch, razing Soba afterwards and enslaving its population.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVantini2006487–491_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVantini2006487–491-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <i><a href="/wiki/Funj_Chronicle" title="Funj Chronicle">Funj Chronicle</a></i>, a multi-authored<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHasan1967213_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasan1967213-117">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> history of the <a href="/wiki/Funj_Sultanate" title="Funj Sultanate">Funj Sultanate</a> compiled in the 19th century, ascribes the destruction of Alodia to King Amara Dunqas; he was also allied with Abdallah Jammah.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdams1977538_112-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams1977538-112">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> This attack is dated to the <a href="/wiki/Hijri_year" title="Hijri year">9th century after the Hijra</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1396–1494). Afterwards, Soba is said to have served as the capital of the Funj until the foundation of Sennar in 1504.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVantini1975786–787_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVantini1975786–787-118">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> The <i><a href="/w/index.php?title=Tabaqat_Dayfallah&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Tabaqat Dayfallah (page does not exist)">Tabaqat Dayfallah</a></i>, a history of <a href="/wiki/Sufism" title="Sufism">Sufism</a> in Sudan (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1700), briefly mentions that the Funj attacked and defeated the "kingdom of the Nuba" in 1504–1505.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVantini1975784–785_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVantini1975784–785-119">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alodia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:King_sennar_1821.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A robed man with a sword" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/a7/King_sennar_1821.jpg" decoding="async" width="171" height="300" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="171" data-file-height="300" /></a><figcaption>Early 19th century <a href="/wiki/Funj_Sultanate" title="Funj Sultanate">Funj</a> <i>manjil</i> ("kinglet") of <a href="/wiki/Fazughli" class="mw-redirect" title="Fazughli">Fazughli</a> as depicted by <a href="/wiki/Fr%C3%A9d%C3%A9ric_Cailliaud" title="Frédéric Cailliaud">Frédéric Cailliaud</a>. On his head he wears a <i>taqiya umm qarnein</i>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Historian <a href="/w/index.php?title=Jay_Spaulding&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Jay Spaulding (page does not exist)">Jay Spaulding</a> proposes that the fall of Soba was not necessarily the end of Alodia. According to the <a href="/wiki/Judaism" title="Judaism">Jewish</a> traveler <a href="/wiki/David_Reubeni" title="David Reubeni">David Reubeni</a>, who visited the country in 1523, there was still a "Kingdom of Soba" on the eastern bank of the Blue Nile, although he explicitly noted Soba itself was in ruins. This matches the oral traditions from the Upper Blue Nile, which claim that Alodia survived Soba's fall and still existed along the Blue Nile. It had gradually retreated to the mountains of <a href="/wiki/Fazughli" class="mw-redirect" title="Fazughli">Fazughli</a> in the Ethiopian-Sudanese borderlands, forming the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Fazughli" title="Kingdom of Fazughli">Kingdom of Fazughli</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESpaulding197412–21_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpaulding197412–21-120">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> Recent excavations in western Ethiopia seem to confirm the theory of an Alodian migration.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGonzalez-RuibalFalquina201716–18_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGonzalez-RuibalFalquina201716–18-121">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> The Funj eventually conquered Fazughli in 1685 and its population, known as <a href="/w/index.php?title=Hamaj&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Hamaj (page does not exist)">Hamaj</a>, became a fundamental part of Sennar, eventually <a href="/wiki/Hamaj_Regency" title="Hamaj Regency">seizing power</a> in 1761–1762.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESpaulding197421–25_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpaulding197421–25-122">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> As recently as 1930<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdams1977539_113-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams1977539-113">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> Hamaj villagers in the southern Gezira would swear by "Soba the home of my grandfathers and grandmothers which can make the stone float and the cotton ball sink".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199199_94-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199199-94">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1504–1505 the Funj founded the Funj sultanate, incorporating Abdallah Jammah's domain, which, according to some traditions, happened after a battle where Amara Dunqas defeated him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;FaheySpaulding197425–26_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;FaheySpaulding197425–26-123">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> The Funj maintained some medieval Nubian customs like the wearing of crowns with features resembling <a href="/wiki/Bovinae" title="Bovinae">bovine</a> horns, called <i>taqiya umm qarnein</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZurawski2014148–149_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZurawski2014148–149-124">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> the shaving of the head of a king upon his coronation,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZurawski2014149_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZurawski2014149-125">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> and, according to Jay Spaulding, the custom of raising princes separately from their mothers, under strict confinement.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESpaulding198523_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpaulding198523-126">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The aftermath of Alodia's fall saw extensive <a href="/wiki/Arabization" title="Arabization">Arabization</a>, with the Nubians embracing the tribal system of the Arab migrants.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013156_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013156-127">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> Those living along the Nile between al Dabbah in the north and the confluence of the two Niles in the south were subsumed into the <a href="/wiki/Ja%27alin_tribe" title="Ja&#39;alin tribe">Ja'alin tribe</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdams1977557–558_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams1977557–558-128">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> To the east, west and south of the Ja'alin the country was now dominated by tribes claiming a <a href="/wiki/Juhaynah" title="Juhaynah">Juhaynah</a> ancestry.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdams1977558_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams1977558-129">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> In the area around Soba, the tribal Abdallab identity prevailed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;FaheySpaulding197429_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;FaheySpaulding197429-130">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> The Nubian language was spoken in central Sudan until the 19th century, when it was replaced by <a href="/wiki/Sudanese_Arabic" title="Sudanese Arabic">Arabic</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards2004260_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards2004260-131">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Sudanese_Arabic" title="Sudanese Arabic">Sudanese Arabic</a> preserves many words of Nubian origin,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAbu-Manga2009377_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbu-Manga2009377-132">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> and Nubian place names can be found as far south as the <a href="/wiki/Blue_Nile_(state)" title="Blue Nile (state)">Blue Nile state</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaha201210_(Taha_ascribes_these_names_a_&#91;&#91;Dongolawi_language&#124;Dongolawi_Nubian&#93;&#93;_origin)_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaha201210_(Taha_ascribes_these_names_a_[[Dongolawi_language|Dongolawi_Nubian]]_origin)-133">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The fate of Christianity in the region remains largely unknown.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013171_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013171-134">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> The church institutions would have collapsed together with the fall of the kingdom,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013156_127-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013156-127">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> resulting in the decline of the Christian faith and the rise of Islam in its stead.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdams1977564_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams1977564-135">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Islamization" class="mw-redirect" title="Islamization">Islamized</a> groups from northern Nubia began to proselytize the Gezira.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcHugh199459_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcHugh199459-136">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> As early as 1523 King Amara Dunqas, who was initially a Pagan or nominal Christian, was recorded to be Muslim.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013170–171_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013170–171-137">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, in the 16th century large portions of the Nubians still regarded themselves as Christians.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZurawski201484–85_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZurawski201484–85-138">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> A traveler who visited Nubia around 1500 confirms this, while also saying that the Nubians were so lacking in Christian instruction they had no knowledge of the faith.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHasan1967131–132_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasan1967131–132-139">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> In 1520 Nubian ambassadors reached Ethiopia and petitioned the Emperor for priests. They claimed that no more priests could reach Nubia because of the wars between Muslims, leading to a decline of Christianity in their land.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013150_140-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013150-140">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> In the first half of the 17th century, a prophecy made by the Sudanese Sheikh Idris Wad al-Arbab mentioned a church in the Nuba Mountains.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013181_141-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013181-141">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> As late as the early 1770s there was said to be a Christian princedom in the Ethiopian-Sudanese border area, called Shaira.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESpaulding197422,_note_31_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpaulding197422,_note_31-142">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Apotropaic_magic" title="Apotropaic magic">Apotropaic rituals</a> stemming from Christian practices outlived the conversion to Islam.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013177_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013177-143">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> As late as the 20th century several practices of undoubtedly Christian origin were "common, though of course not universal, in <a href="/wiki/Omdurman" title="Omdurman">Omdurman</a>, the Gezira and Kordofan",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrowfoot191856_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrowfoot191856-144">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> usually revolving around the application of crosses on humans and objects.<sup id="cite_ref-fn3_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fn3-150">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Soba, which remained inhabited until at least the early 17th century,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrawford195128–29_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrawford195128–29-151">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> served, among many other ruined Alodian sites, as a steady supply of <a href="/wiki/Brick#Fired_brick" title="Brick">bricks</a> and stones for nearby <a href="/wiki/Qubba" title="Qubba">Qubba shrines</a>, dedicated to Sufi holy men.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcHugh2016110_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcHugh2016110-152">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> During the early 19th century many of the remaining bricks in Soba were plundered for the construction of <a href="/wiki/Khartoum" title="Khartoum">Khartoum</a>, the new capital of <a href="/wiki/Turkish_Sudan" class="mw-redirect" title="Turkish Sudan">Turkish Sudan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199143_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199143-153">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Administration">Administration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alodia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Administration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>While information about Alodia's government is sparse,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199197_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199197-154">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> it was likely similar to <a href="/wiki/Makuria#Government" title="Makuria">that of Makuria</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEObluski201716_155-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEObluski201716-155">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> The head of state was the king who, according to al-Aswani, reigned as an <a href="/wiki/Absolute_monarchy" title="Absolute monarchy">absolute monarch</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199197_154-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199197-154">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> He was recorded to be able to enslave any of his subjects at will, who would not oppose his decision, but prostrated themselves before him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVantini1975614_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVantini1975614-156">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> As in Makuria, succession to the Alodian throne was <a href="/wiki/Matrilineal_succession" title="Matrilineal succession">matrilineal</a>: it was the son of the king's sister, not his son who succeeded to the throne.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEObluski201716_155-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEObluski201716-155">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> There might be evidence a mobile royal encampment existed, although the translation of the original source, Abu al-Makarim, is not certain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeignobos2015224_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeignobos2015224-157">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> Similar mobile courts are known to have existed in the early Funj sultanate, Ethiopia and <a href="/wiki/Darfur_Sultanate" class="mw-redirect" title="Darfur Sultanate">Darfur</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESpaulding197252_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESpaulding197252-158">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The kingdom was divided into several provinces under the sovereignty of Soba.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug1991100_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug1991100-159">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> It seems delegates of the king governed these provinces.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199197_154-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199197-154">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> Al-Aswani stated that the governor of the northern al-Abwab province was appointed by the king.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199119_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199119-160">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> This was similar to what Ibn Hawqal recorded for the Gash Delta region, which was ruled by an appointed Arabophone (Arabic speaker).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199198_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199198-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> In 1286, Mamluk emissaries were sent to several rulers in central Sudan. It is unclear whether those rulers were actually independent,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEO&#39;FaheySpaulding197419_79-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO&#39;FaheySpaulding197419-79">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> or if they remained subordinate to the king of Alodia. If the latter was the case, this would provide an understanding of the kingdom's territorial organization. The "<a href="/wiki/Sahib" title="Sahib">Sahib</a>" of al-Abwab<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199199_94-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199199-94">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> seems certain to have been independent.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002254_92-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002254-92">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> Apart from al-Abwab, the following regions are mentioned: Al-Anag (possibly Fazughli); Ari; Barah; Befal; Danfou; Kedru (possibly after Kadero, a village north of Khartoum); Kersa (the Gezira); and Taka (the region around the Gash Delta).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199198–100_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199198–100-161">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>State and church were intertwined in Alodia,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013165_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013165-162">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> with the Alodian kings probably serving as its patrons.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug1991101_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug1991101-163">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> Coptic documents observed by <a href="/wiki/Johann_Michael_Vansleb" title="Johann Michael Vansleb">Johann Michael Vansleb</a> during the later 17th century list the following bishoprics in the Alodian kingdom: Arodias, Borra, Gagara, Martin, Banazi, and Menkesa.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrawford195126_164-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrawford195126-164">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> "Arodias" may refer to the bishopric in Soba.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013165_162-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013165-162">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> The bishops were dependent on the patriarch of Alexandria.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199120_5-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199120-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
