</p><p>Alodia may have had a standing army,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199198–100_161-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199198–100-161">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> in which cavalry likely projected force and symbolized royal authority deep into the provinces.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199122_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199122-165">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> Because of their speed, horses were also important for communication, providing a rapid courier service between the capital and the provinces.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199122_165-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199122-165">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> Aside from horses, boats also played a central role in transportation infrastructure.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199185_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199185-166">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Culture">Culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alodia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Languages">Languages</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alodia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Languages">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Nubian_inscriptions_Musawwarat.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A sketch of Nubian inscriptions" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Nubian_inscriptions_Musawwarat.jpg/220px-Nubian_inscriptions_Musawwarat.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="201" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Nubian_inscriptions_Musawwarat.jpg/330px-Nubian_inscriptions_Musawwarat.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Nubian_inscriptions_Musawwarat.jpg/440px-Nubian_inscriptions_Musawwarat.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1168" data-file-height="1066" /></a><figcaption>Nubian graffiti from Musawwarat es-Sufra</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tombstone_of_king_David.tif" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Tombstone_of_king_David.tif/lossy-page1-220px-Tombstone_of_king_David.tif.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="178" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Tombstone_of_king_David.tif/lossy-page1-330px-Tombstone_of_king_David.tif.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/Tombstone_of_king_David.tif/lossy-page1-440px-Tombstone_of_king_David.tif.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1481" data-file-height="1196" /></a><figcaption>Tombstone of King David (9th or 10th century), carved in the <a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek language</a> at Soba.</figcaption></figure>
<p>While Alodia was polyethnic, and hence polylingual,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199188–90_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199188–90-169">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> it was essentially a Nubian state whose majority spoke a Nubian language.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner201346_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner201346-170">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> Based on a few inscriptions found in Alodian territory it has been suggested that the Alodians spoke a dialect distinct from <a href="/wiki/Old_Nubian" title="Old Nubian">Old Nobiin</a> of northern Nubia, dubbed as <i>Alwan-Nubian</i>. This assumption rests primarily on the script used in these inscriptions,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBreyer2014188–189_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBreyer2014188–189-171">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> which, while also being based on the <a href="/wiki/Greek_alphabet" title="Greek alphabet">Greek alphabet</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013186,_note_6_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013186,_note_6-172">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> differs from that employed in Makuria by making no use of <a href="/wiki/Coptic_alphabet" class="mw-redirect" title="Coptic alphabet">Coptic</a> <a href="/wiki/Diacritic" title="Diacritic">diacritics</a> and instead having special characters based on Meroitic <a href="/wiki/Hieroglyphs" class="mw-redirect" title="Hieroglyphs">hieroglyphs</a>. However, ultimately the classification of this language and its relationship to Old Nobiin has yet to be specified.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBreyer2014189–190_173-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBreyer2014189–190-173">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup>
In the 1830s it was said a Nubian language was still being spoken as far south as <a href="/wiki/Berber,_Sudan" title="Berber, Sudan">Berber</a> near the junction of the Nile and the Atbara. It was supposedly similar to <a href="/wiki/Kenzi_language" title="Kenzi language">Kenzi</a> but with many differences.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERussegger1843456_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERussegger1843456-174">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Marble_stone_with_southern-Nubian_inscription,_Soba.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A drawing of fragments of marble bearing inscriptions in Nubian" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Marble_stone_with_southern-Nubian_inscription%2C_Soba.jpg/240px-Marble_stone_with_southern-Nubian_inscription%2C_Soba.jpg" decoding="async" width="240" height="172" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Marble_stone_with_southern-Nubian_inscription%2C_Soba.jpg/360px-Marble_stone_with_southern-Nubian_inscription%2C_Soba.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/Marble_stone_with_southern-Nubian_inscription%2C_Soba.jpg/480px-Marble_stone_with_southern-Nubian_inscription%2C_Soba.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="1430" /></a><figcaption>Fragmentary marble stone from Soba with Nubian inscription</figcaption></figure>
<p>Although Greek, a prestigious sacral language, was used, it does not appear to have been spoken.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOchala201443–44_175-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOchala201443–44-175">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> An example of the use of Greek in Alodia is the tombstone of King David from Soba, where it is written with quite correct grammar.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels1991274–276_176-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels1991274–276-176">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> Al-Aswani noted that books were written in Greek and then translated into Nubian.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199120_5-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199120-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> The Christian <a href="/wiki/Liturgy" title="Liturgy">liturgy</a> was also in Greek.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013197_177-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013197-177">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Coptic_language" title="Coptic language">Coptic</a> was probably used to communicate with the Patriarch of Alexandria,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEObluski201716_155-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEObluski201716-155">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> but written Coptic remains are very sparse.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOchala201437_178-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOchala201437-178">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Apart from Nubian, a multitude of languages were spoken throughout the kingdom. In the Nuba mountains several <a href="/wiki/Kordofanian_languages" title="Kordofanian languages">Kordofanian languages</a> occurred together with <a href="/wiki/Hill_Nubian_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Hill Nubian language">Hill Nubian</a> dialects. Upstream along the Blue Nile Eastern Sudanic languages like <a href="/wiki/Berta_language" title="Berta language">Berta</a> or <a href="/wiki/Gumuz_language" title="Gumuz language">Gumuz</a> were spoken. In the eastern territories lived the Beja, who spoke their own <a href="/wiki/Cushitic_languages" title="Cushitic languages">Cushitic</a> <a href="/wiki/Beja_language" title="Beja language">language</a>, as did the <a href="/wiki/Semitic_languages" title="Semitic languages">Semitic</a> Arabs<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199189–90_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199189–90-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Tigre_people" title="Tigre people">Tigre</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZaborski2003471_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZaborski2003471-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Church_architecture">Church architecture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alodia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Church architecture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ground_plan_of_Mound_C_church,_Soba.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Ground plan of church complex" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Ground_plan_of_Mound_C_church%2C_Soba.jpg/190px-Ground_plan_of_Mound_C_church%2C_Soba.jpg" decoding="async" width="190" height="134" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Ground_plan_of_Mound_C_church%2C_Soba.jpg/285px-Ground_plan_of_Mound_C_church%2C_Soba.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Ground_plan_of_Mound_C_church%2C_Soba.jpg/380px-Ground_plan_of_Mound_C_church%2C_Soba.jpg 2x" data-file-width="872" data-file-height="613" /></a><figcaption>Suggestive ground plan of the "Mound C" church, Soba</figcaption></figure>
<p>The existence of 400 churches has been recorded throughout the kingdom; most have yet to be located.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002153_179-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002153-179">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> Only seven have been identified so far, given the simple names of church "A", "B", "C", "E", the "Mound C" church in Soba, the church in Saqadi and the temple-church in Musawwarat as-Sufra.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002149,_note_38_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002149,_note_38-180">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> A hypothetical church was recently discovered in Abu Erteila in the western Butana.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaldiVarriale2010284–288_181-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaldiVarriale2010284–288-181">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup> Churches "A"–"C" as well as the "Mound C" church were <a href="/wiki/Basilica" title="Basilica">basilicas</a> comparable to the largest Makurian churches. The Saqadi church was an insertion into a pre-existing structure. Church "E" and the church of Musawwarat es-Sufra were "normal" churches. Thus, the known Alodian houses of worship can be categorized into three classes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002153_179-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002153-179">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Soba_3_churches.gif" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Ground plan of church" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Soba_3_churches.gif/180px-Soba_3_churches.gif" decoding="async" width="180" height="348" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Soba_3_churches.gif/270px-Soba_3_churches.gif 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e8/Soba_3_churches.gif/360px-Soba_3_churches.gif 2x" data-file-width="781" data-file-height="1509" /></a><figcaption>Church complex of "Mound B", Soba, including, from up to bottom: "Church A", "Church B" and "Church C"</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Small_finds_from_Soba_(Duemichen).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Pen and ink drawing of ornamented capitals" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9f/Small_finds_from_Soba_%28Duemichen%29.jpg/280px-Small_finds_from_Soba_%28Duemichen%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="280" height="191" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9f/Small_finds_from_Soba_%28Duemichen%29.jpg/420px-Small_finds_from_Soba_%28Duemichen%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9f/Small_finds_from_Soba_%28Duemichen%29.jpg/560px-Small_finds_from_Soba_%28Duemichen%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3872" data-file-height="2648" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Capital_(architecture)" title="Capital (architecture)">Capitals</a> and other small finds from Soba</figcaption></figure>
<p>On "Mound B" in Soba lay the standalone complex of the three churches "A", "B" and "C". Churches "A" and "B", both probably built in the mid-9th century, were large buildings, the first measuring 28&#160;m ×&#160;24.5&#160;m (92&#160;ft ×&#160;80&#160;ft) and the second 27&#160;m ×&#160;22.5&#160;m (89&#160;ft ×&#160;74&#160;ft). Church "C" was much smaller <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013163_182-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013163-182">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> and built after the other two churches, probably after <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 900.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels199134_80-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels199134-80">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> The three churches had many similarities, including having a <a href="/wiki/Narthex" title="Narthex">narthex</a>, wide entrances on the main east-west axis and a <a href="/wiki/Pulpit" title="Pulpit">pulpit</a> along the north side of the <a href="/wiki/Nave" title="Nave">nave</a>. Differences are evident in the thickness of the bricks used. Church "C" lacked outer <a href="/wiki/Aisle" title="Aisle">aisles</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby1996188_183-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby1996188-183">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> It seems probable that the complex was the ecclesiastical center of Soba, if not the entire kingdom.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards2004222_184-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards2004222-184">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Temple_3_A,_Musawwarat_es-Sufra.png" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Ground plan of church" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Temple_3_A%2C_Musawwarat_es-Sufra.png/190px-Temple_3_A%2C_Musawwarat_es-Sufra.png" decoding="async" width="190" height="190" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Temple_3_A%2C_Musawwarat_es-Sufra.png/285px-Temple_3_A%2C_Musawwarat_es-Sufra.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/fd/Temple_3_A%2C_Musawwarat_es-Sufra.png 2x" data-file-width="330" data-file-height="330" /></a><figcaption>Temple-church, Musawwarat es-Sufra</figcaption></figure>
<p>Church "E", on a natural mount, was 16.4&#160;m ×&#160;10.6&#160;m (54&#160;ft ×&#160;35&#160;ft) in size (and like all red brick structures in Soba heavily robbed).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby199828–29_185-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby199828–29-185">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> Its layout was unusual,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002154_186-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002154-186">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> such as its L-shaped narthex.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby1998275_187-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby1998275-187">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> The roof was supported by wooden beams resting on stone <a href="/wiki/Pedestal" title="Pedestal">pedestals</a>. The internal walls used to be covered by painted whitewashed mud; the external walls were rendered in white lime mortar.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby199830–32_188-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby199830–32-188">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The "Mound C" church, perhaps the oldest of the churches of Soba,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby1996187_189-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby1996187-189">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> was around 13.5&#160;m (44&#160;ft) in length. It was the only Alodian church known to have incorporated stone columns.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002153_179-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002153-179">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> Very little remains of it and its walls, probably made of red bricks, have completely disappeared. Five <a href="/wiki/Capital_(architecture)" title="Capital (architecture)">capitals</a> have been noted, belonging to a style that appeared in Nubia at the turn of the 8th century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels1991321–322_190-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels1991321–322-190">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ruins_uncovered_at_Saqadi,_1913_Wellcome_M0013045.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A black and white photograph of an archaeological excavation site" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Ruins_uncovered_at_Saqadi%2C_1913_Wellcome_M0013045.jpg/220px-Ruins_uncovered_at_Saqadi%2C_1913_Wellcome_M0013045.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="149" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Ruins_uncovered_at_Saqadi%2C_1913_Wellcome_M0013045.jpg/330px-Ruins_uncovered_at_Saqadi%2C_1913_Wellcome_M0013045.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Ruins_uncovered_at_Saqadi%2C_1913_Wellcome_M0013045.jpg/440px-Ruins_uncovered_at_Saqadi%2C_1913_Wellcome_M0013045.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4000" data-file-height="2708" /></a><figcaption>The unearthing of the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Saqadi&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Saqadi (page does not exist)">Saqadi</a> church in 1913</figcaption></figure>
<p>The church of Musawwarat es-Sufra, called "Temple III A", was initially a pagan temple but was converted into a church, probably soon after the royal conversion in 580.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETörök1974100_191-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETörök1974100-191">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> It was rectangular and slightly skewed, being 8.6&#160;m–8.8&#160;m ×&#160;7.4&#160;m–7.6&#160;m (28&#160;ft–29&#160;ft ×&#160;24&#160;ft–25&#160;ft) in size. It was divided into one large and three small rooms.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002154_186-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002154-186">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> The roof, of an indeterminate shape, was supported by wooden beams.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETörök197495_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETörök197495-192">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> Despite originally being a Kushite temple it still bears similarities to purpose-built churches, for example having an entrance on both the north and south sides.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002154_186-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002154-186">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The southernmost known Nubian church was in Saqadi,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby200286_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby200286-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> a red brick building<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels1991322_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels1991322-193">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> inserted into a pre-existing building of unknown nature.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002153_179-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002153-179">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> It had a nave, where two L-shaped walls projected, and at least two <a href="/wiki/Aisle#Church_architecture" title="Aisle">aisles</a> with rectangular brick piers between, as well as a range of possibly three rooms across the western end, which was a typically Nubian arrangement.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels1991322_193-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels1991322-193">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nubian church architecture was greatly influenced by that of Egypt, <a href="/wiki/Syria" title="Syria">Syria</a> and <a href="/wiki/Armenia" title="Armenia">Armenia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002155_194-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002155-194">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> The constellation of the "Mound B" complex might reflect Byzantine influences.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013164_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013164-195">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> The relations between the church architecture of Makuria and Alodia remain uncertain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002149_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002149-196">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> What seems clear is that Alodian churches lacked eastern entrances and <a href="/wiki/Tribune_(architecture)" title="Tribune (architecture)">tribunes</a>, features characteristic for churches in northern Nubia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby1996189_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby1996189-197">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> Furthermore, Alodian churches used more wood.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013164_195-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013164-195">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> Similarities with medieval Ethiopian church architecture are harder to find, only a few details matching.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels1991322_193-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels1991322-193">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Pottery">Pottery</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alodia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Pottery">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In medieval Nubia, pottery and its decoration were appreciated as an art form.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002194_198-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002194-198">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> Until the 7th century, the most common pottery type found at Soba was the so-called "Red Ware". These <a href="/wiki/Potter%27s_wheel" title="Potter&#39;s wheel">wheel-made</a> hemispherical bowls were made of red or orange <a href="/wiki/Slip_(ceramics)" title="Slip (ceramics)">slip</a> and painted with separated motifs such as boxes with inner cross-hatchings, stylized floral motifs or crosses. The outlines of the motifs were drawn in black while the interiors were white. In their design, they are a direct continuation of Kushite styles, with possible influences from Aksumite Ethiopia. Due to their relative rarity, it has been suggested that they were imported, although they bear similarities to the pottery type, known as "<a href="/w/index.php?title=Soba_Ware&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Soba Ware (page does not exist)">Soba Ware</a>", that succeeded them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDanysZielinska2017177–178_199-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDanysZielinska2017177–178-199">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>"Soba Ware" was a type of wheel-made<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDanysZielinska2017182_200-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDanysZielinska2017182-200">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> pottery with a distinctive decoration very different from that found in the rest of Nubia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002234_201-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002234-201">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> The shape of the pottery was diverse, as was the repertoire of painted decoration. One of the most distinctive features was the use of faces as painted decoration. They were simplified, if not geometric, in form and with big round eyes. This style is foreign to Makuria and Egypt, but bears a resemblance to paintings and manuscripts from Ethiopia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDanysZielinska2017179–181_202-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDanysZielinska2017179–181-202">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> It is possible the potters copied these motifs from local church murals.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002235_203-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002235-203">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> Also unique was the application of animal-shaped bosses (<a href="/wiki/Protome" title="Protome">protomes</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDanysZielinska2017180_204-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDanysZielinska2017180-204">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Ceramic_glaze" title="Ceramic glaze">Glazed</a> vessels were also produced, copying <a href="/wiki/Persia" class="mw-redirect" title="Persia">Persian</a> <a href="/wiki/Aquamanile" title="Aquamanile">aquamaniles</a> without reaching their quality.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002194–195_205-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002194–195-205">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> Beginning in the 9th century, "Soba Ware" was increasingly replaced by fine ware imported from Makuria.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDanysZielinska2017183_206-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDanysZielinska2017183-206">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Economy">Economy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alodia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Economy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Agriculture">Agriculture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alodia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Agriculture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:L%27univers_S%C3%A9n%C3%A9gambie_et_Guin%C3%A9e_(...)Tardieu_Am%C3%A9d%C3%A9e_bpt6k6209333w_621.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Old drawing of a sakia waterwheel" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/L%27univers_S%C3%A9n%C3%A9gambie_et_Guin%C3%A9e_%28...%29Tardieu_Am%C3%A9d%C3%A9e_bpt6k6209333w_621.jpg/220px-L%27univers_S%C3%A9n%C3%A9gambie_et_Guin%C3%A9e_%28...%29Tardieu_Am%C3%A9d%C3%A9e_bpt6k6209333w_621.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/L%27univers_S%C3%A9n%C3%A9gambie_et_Guin%C3%A9e_%28...%29Tardieu_Am%C3%A9d%C3%A9e_bpt6k6209333w_621.jpg/330px-L%27univers_S%C3%A9n%C3%A9gambie_et_Guin%C3%A9e_%28...%29Tardieu_Am%C3%A9d%C3%A9e_bpt6k6209333w_621.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/L%27univers_S%C3%A9n%C3%A9gambie_et_Guin%C3%A9e_%28...%29Tardieu_Am%C3%A9d%C3%A9e_bpt6k6209333w_621.jpg/440px-L%27univers_S%C3%A9n%C3%A9gambie_et_Guin%C3%A9e_%28...%29Tardieu_Am%C3%A9d%C3%A9e_bpt6k6209333w_621.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="534" /></a><figcaption>A Nubian <a href="/wiki/Saqiyah" title="Saqiyah">sakia</a> in the 19th century</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sorghum_sudanese.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A close up photograph of a fully grown sorghum plant" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Sorghum_sudanese.jpg/180px-Sorghum_sudanese.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="240" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Sorghum_sudanese.jpg/270px-Sorghum_sudanese.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Sorghum_sudanese.jpg/360px-Sorghum_sudanese.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1952" data-file-height="2608" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Sorghum" title="Sorghum">Sorghum</a> was the <a href="/wiki/Staple_food" title="Staple food">staple food</a> of medieval Nubia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002185_207-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002185-207">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Alodia was in the <a href="/wiki/Sahel" title="Sahel">savannah belt</a>, giving it an economic advantage over its northern neighbor Makuria.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2014183_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2014183-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> According to al-Aswani the "provisions of the country of Alwa and their king" came from Kersa, which has been identified with the Gezira.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug1991100_159-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug1991100-159">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> North of the confluence of the two Niles agriculture was limited to farms along the river<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199174_30-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199174-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> watered by devices like the <a href="/wiki/Shadoof" title="Shadoof">shadoof</a> or the more sophisticated <a href="/wiki/Sakia" class="mw-redirect" title="Sakia">sakia</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199177–79_208-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199177–79-208">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> In contrast, the farmers of the Gezira profited from sufficient rainfall to make rainfall cultivation the economic mainstay.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199175_209-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199175-209">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup>
Archaeological records have provided insight into the types of food grown and consumed in Alodia. At Soba, the primary cereal was <a href="/wiki/Sorghum" title="Sorghum">sorghum</a>, although <a href="/wiki/Barley" title="Barley">barley</a> and <a href="/wiki/Millet" title="Millet">millet</a> were also known to be consumed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels1991265–267_210-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels1991265–267-210">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup> Al-Aswani noted that sorghum was used to make <a href="/wiki/Beer" title="Beer">beer</a> and said that <a href="/wiki/Vineyard" title="Vineyard">vineyards</a> were quite rare in Alodia compared to Makuria.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVantini1975613_211-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVantini1975613-211">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> There is archaeological evidence of grapes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002186_212-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002186-212">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> According to al-Idrisi, <a href="/wiki/Onion" title="Onion">onions</a>, <a href="/wiki/Horseradish" title="Horseradish">horseradish</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cucumber" title="Cucumber">cucumbers</a>, <a href="/wiki/Watermelon" title="Watermelon">watermelons</a> and <a href="/wiki/Rapeseed" title="Rapeseed">rapeseed</a> were also cultivated,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVantini1975274_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVantini1975274-213">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> but none were found at Soba.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels1991273_214-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels1991273-214">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup> Instead, <a href="/wiki/Common_fig" class="mw-redirect" title="Common fig">figs</a>, <a href="/wiki/Acacia" title="Acacia">acacia fruits</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hyphaene_thebaica" title="Hyphaene thebaica">doum palm fruits</a> and <a href="/wiki/Date_(fruit)" class="mw-redirect" title="Date (fruit)">dates</a> have been identified.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels1991Table_16_215-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels1991Table_16-215">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Sedentary farmers formed one part of Alodia's agriculture, the other consisted of nomads practicing animal husbandry.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEObluski201716_155-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEObluski201716-155">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> The relationship between these two groups was symbiotic, resulting in an exchange of goods.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002188_216-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002188-216">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> Al-Aswani wrote that <a href="/wiki/Beef" title="Beef">beef</a> was plentiful in Alodia, which he attributed to the bountiful grazing land.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVantini1975614_156-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVantini1975614-156">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> Archaeological evidence from Soba attests to the relevance <a href="/wiki/Cattle" title="Cattle">cattle</a> had there,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby1998245_217-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby1998245-217">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> as most animal bones are attributed to that species, followed by those of <a href="/wiki/Sheep" title="Sheep">sheep</a> and <a href="/wiki/Goat" title="Goat">goats</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002187_218-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002187-218">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Domesticated_chicken" class="mw-redirect" title="Domesticated chicken">Chickens</a> were probably also bred at Soba,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby1998245_217-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby1998245-217">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> although available archaeological proof is very limited, probably due to the fragile nature of bird bones.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby1998241_219-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby1998241-219">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup> No remains of <a href="/wiki/Domestic_pig" class="mw-redirect" title="Domestic pig">pigs</a> have been identified.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002187_218-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002187-218">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> Camel remains have been noted, but none bore signs of butchery.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby1998240_220-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby1998240-220">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup> Fishing and hunting made only minor contributions to the overall diet of Soba.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002188_216-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002188-216">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Trade">Trade</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alodia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Trade">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Trade was an important source of income for the people of Alodia. Soba served as a trading hub with north-south and east-west trade routes; goods arrived in the kingdom from Makuria, the <a href="/wiki/Middle_East" title="Middle East">Middle East</a>, western Africa, <a href="/wiki/India" title="India">India</a> and <a href="/wiki/China" title="China">China</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013166_221-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013166-221">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup> Trade with Makuria probably ran through the <a href="/wiki/Bayuda_Desert" title="Bayuda Desert">Bayuda Desert</a>, following <a href="/wiki/Wadi_Abu_Dom" title="Wadi Abu Dom">Wadi Abu Dom</a> or <a href="/wiki/Wadi_Muqaddam" title="Wadi Muqaddam">Wadi Muqaddam</a>, while another route went from near Abu Hamad to <a href="/wiki/Korosko" title="Korosko">Korosko</a> in <a href="/wiki/Lower_Nubia" title="Lower Nubia">Lower Nubia</a>. A route going east originated around Berber near the confluence of the Nile and the Atbara, terminating in Badi, Suakin and <a href="/wiki/Dahlak_archipelago" class="mw-redirect" title="Dahlak archipelago">Dahlak</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002213_222-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002213-222">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> Merchant <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_of_Tudela" title="Benjamin of Tudela">Benjamin of Tudela</a> mentions a route heading west, going from Alodia to <a href="/wiki/Zwila" class="mw-redirect" title="Zwila">Zuwila</a> in <a href="/wiki/Fezzan" title="Fezzan">Fezzan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199187_223-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199187-223">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup> Archaeological evidence for trade with Ethiopia is virtually absent,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002214–215_224-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002214–215-224">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup> although trading relations are suggested by other evidence.<sup id="cite_ref-fn4_229-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fn4-229">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> Trading with the outside world was handled predominantly by Arab merchants.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199186_230-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199186-230">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup> Muslim merchants were recorded as having traversed Nubia, some living in a district in Soba.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHasan196746_231-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasan196746-231">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Exports from Alodia likely included raw materials such as <a href="/wiki/Gold" title="Gold">gold</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ivory" title="Ivory">ivory</a>, <a href="/wiki/Salt" title="Salt">salt</a> and other tropical products,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199184_232-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199184-232">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> as well as hides.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199182_233-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199182-233">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> According to an oral tradition Arab merchants came to Alodia to sell silk and textiles, receiving beads, elephant teeth and leather in return.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAbd_ar-Rahman201152_234-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbd_ar-Rahman201152-234">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup> At Soba silk and flax have been found, both probably originating from Egypt.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels1991307_235-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels1991307-235">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup> Most of the glass found there was also imported.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels19919_81-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsbyDaniels19919-81">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> Benjamin of Tudela claimed merchants traveling from Alodia to Zuwila carried hides, wheat, fruits, legumes and salt, while carrying gold and precious stones on their return.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHess196517_236-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHess196517-236">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup>
Slaves are commonly assumed to have been exported by medieval Nubia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards201187–88_237-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards201187–88-237">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup> Adams postulates that Alodia was a specialized slave-trading state that exploited the pagan populations to the west and south.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdams1977471_238-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams1977471-238">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup> Evidence for a regulated slave trade is very limited.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards2011103_239-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards2011103-239">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-fn5_241-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fn5-241">&#91;e&#93;</a></sup> It is only from the 16th century, after the fall of the Christian kingdoms, that such evidence begins to appear.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards201195–96_242-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards201195–96-242">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alodia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-fn1-3"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-fn1_3-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="/wiki/Kordofanian_languages" title="Kordofanian languages">Kordofanian languages</a>; various <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Sudanic_languages" title="Eastern Sudanic languages">Eastern Sudanic languages</a> spoken in the <a href="/wiki/Blue_Nile" title="Blue Nile">Upper Blue Nile Valley</a> (for example <a href="/wiki/Berta_language" title="Berta language">Berta</a>); <a href="/wiki/Arabic" title="Arabic">Arabic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Beja_language" title="Beja language">Beja</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZarroug199189–90_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZarroug199189–90-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Tigre_language" title="Tigre language">Tigre</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZaborski2003471_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZaborski2003471-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-fn2-27"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-fn2_27-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">"The most southerly church known, which presumably was within the kingdom of Alwa, lay at Saqadi 50 km to the west of Sennar",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby200286_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby200286-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> while "the most southerly find of Alwan material on the Blue Nile is a pottery chalice, from Khalil el-Kubra 40 km upstream of Sennar".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2014185_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2014185-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-fn3-150"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-fn3_150-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">In 1918 it was recorded that in parts of Omdurman, the Gezira and Kordofan, practices of Christian origin included the marking of crosses on foreheads of newborns or on stomachs of sick boys as well as putting straw crosses on bowls of milk.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECrowfoot191855–56_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrowfoot191855–56-145">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> In 1927 it was recorded that along the White Nile, crosses were painted on bowls filled with wheat.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013177–178_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013177–178-146">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> In 1930 it was not only recorded that youths in Fazughli and the Gezira would be painted with crosses, but also that coins with crosses were worn to provide assistance against illnesses.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChataway1930256_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChataway1930256-147">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> A very similar custom was known from Lower Nubia, where women wore such coins on special holidays. It seems likely that this was a living memory of the <a href="/wiki/Jizya" title="Jizya">Jizya</a> tax, which was enforced on Christians who refused to convert to Islam.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013178_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013178-148">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> Christianizing rituals are also known from the Nuba mountains: crosses were painted on foreheads and breasts and were applied to blankets and baskets.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWerner2013182_149-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWerner2013182-149">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-fn4-229"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-fn4_229-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">John of Ephesus wrote of Aksumites in Alodia, possibly referring to merchants,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHatke2013§5.3_225-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHatke2013§5.3-225">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup> while the contemporary <a href="/wiki/Cosmas_Indicopleustes" title="Cosmas Indicopleustes">Cosmas Indicopleustes</a> reported Aksumite trade expeditions into the Blue Nile Valley, so arguably in the Alodian sphere of influence. In the 12th century al-Idrisi made mention of a trading town in the northern Butana, a place "where merchants from Nubia and Ethiopia gather together with those from Egypt".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002215_226-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelsby2002215-226">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> Historian <a href="/w/index.php?title=Mordechai_Abir&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Mordechai Abir (page does not exist)">Mordechai Abir</a> suggests that merchants from the <a href="/wiki/Zagwe_dynasty" title="Zagwe dynasty">Zagwe kingdom</a> traveled through Alodia to reach Egypt.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAbir198015_227-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbir198015-227">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup> Some Ethiopian traditions recall a people named "<i>Soba Noba</i>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrita2014517_228-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrita2014517-228">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-fn5-241"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-fn5_241-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The African slave armies that were deployed in Egypt by the <a href="/wiki/Tulunids" title="Tulunids">Tulunids</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ikhshidids" class="mw-redirect" title="Ikhshidids">Ikhshidids</a> and <a href="/wiki/Fatimids" class="mw-redirect" title="Fatimids">Fatimids</a> are often cited as evidence for a Nubian slave trade, but it is more likely these slaves came from the <a href="/wiki/Chad_basin" class="mw-redirect" title="Chad basin">Chad basin</a> instead. (In Fatimid sources they appear as <i>Zuwayla</i>, indicating an origin from Zuwila in Fezzan.)<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards201189–90_240-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards201189–90-240">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="References">References</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alodia&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: References">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
